Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.

And I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.

They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.

I mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.

They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.

It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.

But my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.

It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.\n\nAnd I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: What do the voices say to you, are they telling you to do something or commenting on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F25.1, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.\n\nAnd I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: What do the voices say to you, are they telling you to do something or commenting on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. It’s not like normal “had coffee” energy. It feels important.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me to slow down, or one of the nurses acts like I’m overdoing it, I get… really sharp with them. I snap, because it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something that matters. And then later I know I sounded awful, but in the moment it’s like, why can’t everyone just move?\n\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. I know that sounds… dramatic, maybe, but it feels real when it’s happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.

And then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.
=====================================

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.

And I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.\n\nAnd I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: What do the voices say to you, are they telling you to do something or commenting on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. It’s not like normal “had coffee” energy. It feels important.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me to slow down, or one of the nurses acts like I’m overdoing it, I get… really sharp with them. I snap, because it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something that matters. And then later I know I sounded awful, but in the moment it’s like, why can’t everyone just move?\n\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. I know that sounds… dramatic, maybe, but it feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.\n\nAnd I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. I know that sounds… dramatic, maybe, but it feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like separate times, I think. Like I can be mostly normal, doing my usual routine, and then some days it’s like a switch flips and I’m finally awake and I can see all the things that are wrong at work and I have to fix them.

And then—this is where it gets messy—if my husband says I need to slow down, or one of the nurses tells me I’m doing too much, I just snap. Like they’re blocking something important. I don’t mean to turn it into a whole thing, but in that moment it feels like I’m the only one seeing it clearly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.\n\nAnd I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: What do the voices say to you, are they telling you to do something or commenting on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. I feel like my work reputation and my plans for LPN school are falling apart because of this.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since I was asked to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / nursing_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S020\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.\n\nAnd I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these times when you're extremely awake and can see what's wrong are separate episodes, or is it more of a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: It feels more like separate times, I think. Like I can be mostly normal, doing my usual routine, and then some days it’s like a switch flips and I’m finally awake and I can see all the things that are wrong at work and I have to fix them.\n\nAnd then—this is where it gets messy—if my husband says I need to slow down, or one of the nurses tells me I’m doing too much, I just snap. Like they’re blocking something important. I don’t mean to turn it into a whole thing, but in that moment it feels like I’m the only one seeing it clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring those times when you feel extremely awake and can see what's wrong, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

I’m not really sure. Sleep is… it’s hard to separate out, because some nights I’m just up thinking about work stuff or what I forgot to do, and then other nights I crash early because I’m exhausted from being on my feet all day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. It comes through the call-light speaker, and sometimes from the hallway vents, telling me which patient records got changed. It sounds so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me.\n\nAnd I know how the vents carry sound sometimes—my old apartment did that too, you could hear the neighbors arguing through the bathroom wall—but this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. That’s what’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: What do the voices say to you, are they telling you to do something or commenting on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: They mostly tell me which patient records have been changed—like, “this chart was altered” or “look at that one,” coming out of the call-light speaker or the vents. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. I don’t know that they’re telling me to hurt anybody or anything like that… it’s more like they’re telling me about the records.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever criticize you or tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Not really criticizing me, no… it’s more like they’re pointing things out, like “that record was changed,” or “check that chart.” It comes through the call-light speaker or the vents, clear like somebody standing right beside me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange, because hospitals are noisy places—machines humming, people talking down the hall, air vents rattling. My old apartment had vents like that too, you could hear people’s TV sometimes. But this is different. The unit can be quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it. It’s mostly about which patient records got changed.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go… it’s not every single second. But when it happens, it feels very clear, like it’s coming right out of the call-light speaker or the hallway vents, telling me which patient records were changed.\n\nSometimes the unit is quiet—like really quiet—and nobody else turns their head or says anything, but I hear it like someone is standing next to me. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. It’s not like normal “had coffee” energy. It feels important.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me to slow down, or one of the nurses acts like I’m overdoing it, I get… really sharp with them. I snap, because it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something that matters. And then later I know I sounded awful, but in the moment it’s like, why can’t everyone just move?\n\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. I know that sounds… dramatic, maybe, but it feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.\n\nAnd I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these times when you're extremely awake and can see what's wrong are separate episodes, or is it more of a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: It feels more like separate times, I think. Like I can be mostly normal, doing my usual routine, and then some days it’s like a switch flips and I’m finally awake and I can see all the things that are wrong at work and I have to fix them.\n\nAnd then—this is where it gets messy—if my husband says I need to slow down, or one of the nurses tells me I’m doing too much, I just snap. Like they’re blocking something important. I don’t mean to turn it into a whole thing, but in that moment it feels like I’m the only one seeing it clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel extremely awake and can see what's wrong, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Sleep is… it’s hard to separate out, because some nights I’m just up thinking about work stuff or what I forgot to do, and then other nights I crash early because I’m exhausted from being on my feet all day.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I need less sleep exactly. My husband might have a better answer because he notices things I don’t, like if I’m pacing around or doing laundry at weird times—well, not laundry, I mean sometimes I’ll start sorting things and then remember there’s a uniform shirt missing… sorry. I guess I haven’t really tracked it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

My husband might have a better answer because he notices things I don’t, like if I’m pacing around or doing laundry at weird times—well, not laundry, I mean sometimes I’ll start sorting things and then remember there’s a uniform shirt missing… sorry. I guess I haven’t really tracked it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because at work I keep hearing voices, and it’s not like the usual noises on the unit. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else seems to hear it, but I do. It’s so clear, like someone’s right next to me.\n\nI mean, I know buildings make noises… at my old place you could hear people through the walls, especially by the bathroom pipes, and at the hospital there’s always humming and beeping and people talking. But this happens even when the unit is quiet, and nobody else reacts. It reminds me a little of how old buildings carry sound… my old apartment had vents where you could hear odd things through the walls, but this feels different. It scares me because it’s so specific.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you hear these voices telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: I feel… scared, mostly. On edge. Because it’s so clear, like somebody is standing right next to me, and it’s coming from the call-light speaker or the vents, telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd I keep thinking, well, hospitals have noises, right? The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. It’s not like normal “had coffee” energy. It feels important.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me to slow down, or one of the nurses acts like I’m overdoing it, I get… really sharp with them. I snap, because it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something that matters. And then later I know I sounded awful, but in the moment it’s like, why can’t everyone just move?\n\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. The air system, the call lights, people talking at the desk—when I worked the floor you learn every little sound. But then the unit will be quiet, nobody else reacts, and I’m still hearing it. That’s when I get really unsettled, like… why am I the only one hearing this?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to act on what the voices are telling you about the patient records?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like they’re telling me “go do this exact thing,” but I feel like I have to do something with it. I’ve been staying after my shifts, going back through things, making binders and checklists for the rollout because it feels urgent—like if I don’t get the whole facility corrected, someone could get hurt.\n\nAnd I know people probably think I’m overdoing it. I’ve called coworkers on their days off too, which… I don’t usually like bothering people, but if a record is wrong, that follows a patient. I remember when we switched forms years ago, everybody hated the new system and half the staff put things in the wrong place, so I keep thinking this could be like that, except worse. So yeah, I guess I do feel like I need to act on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that other people do not see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things… I don’t know, not really like pictures or people standing there. It’s more that I hear it — the call-light speaker, the hallway vents — like someone is right next to me telling me which patient records got changed.\n\nThe vents always bother me, honestly. Old buildings do that, carry sound in strange ways… my apartment used to have this radiator that made a clicking noise all night, and I’d think someone was in the wall. But at work, this is different. The unit can be completely quiet, and nobody else even looks up, but I hear it clear. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed exactly. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down, sure, but that’s different, isn’t it? Like when you’ve worked too many shifts and your feet hurt and then the laundry is still sitting there when you get home… that can make anybody feel low.\n\nSad, maybe sometimes. But I haven’t really been thinking of it that way. It’s more like I’m tired of everything being so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships since these voices started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s affected things. I don’t like admitting that because I’ve always been reliable at work. Like, I’m the one who remembers which patient likes the extra blanket and who needs their water in reach, little things like that.\n\nBut my supervisor actually took me off the floor during a shift. She said my handoffs and charting weren’t safe anymore, which… hearing that was awful. I kept trying to explain that I was double-checking because the records were wrong, but it just made me look worse, I think.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up stuff I usually handle—bills, groceries, appointments, all the normal things. I was supposed to be getting myself ready for LPN school too, and now it feels like my whole work reputation is falling apart. Like all those years of being dependable just got erased.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, there are days where I feel like I’m finally awake, like everything at work suddenly makes sense and I’m supposed to fix it before it gets worse. It’s not like normal “had coffee” energy. It feels important.\n\nBut then if my husband tells me to slow down, or one of the nurses acts like I’m overdoing it, I get… really sharp with them. I snap, because it feels like they’re blocking me from doing something that matters. And then later I know I sounded awful, but in the moment it’s like, why can’t everyone just move?\n\nDoctor: Have you recently experienced a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated, even if things weren't going well?\n\nPatient: Happy isn’t exactly the word I’d use… it’s more like some days I feel suddenly awake, like I can see what’s wrong and I’m the one who’s supposed to fix everything at work.\n\nAnd then if my husband says, “slow down,” or a nurse tells me I’m doing too much, I get mad fast. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. I know that sounds… dramatic, maybe, but it feels real when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Several days… I don’t know if I’d call it happy, exactly. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.\n\nAnd I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these times when you're extremely awake and can see what's wrong are separate episodes, or is it more of a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: It feels more like separate times, I think. Like I can be mostly normal, doing my usual routine, and then some days it’s like a switch flips and I’m finally awake and I can see all the things that are wrong at work and I have to fix them.\n\nAnd then—this is where it gets messy—if my husband says I need to slow down, or one of the nurses tells me I’m doing too much, I just snap. Like they’re blocking something important. I don’t mean to turn it into a whole thing, but in that moment it feels like I’m the only one seeing it clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel extremely awake and can see what's wrong, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Sleep is… it’s hard to separate out, because some nights I’m just up thinking about work stuff or what I forgot to do, and then other nights I crash early because I’m exhausted from being on my feet all day.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I need less sleep exactly. My husband might have a better answer because he notices things I don’t, like if I’m pacing around or doing laundry at weird times—well, not laundry, I mean sometimes I’ll start sorting things and then remember there’s a uniform shirt missing… sorry. I guess I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep on the days when you feel extremely awake and like you can see what's wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it less sleep. It’s more like… I don’t notice sleep the same way on those days, if that makes sense. I’ll be thinking about work and what needs to be corrected, and then I look at the clock and it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut then sometimes I still feel tired, or I’ll tell myself I’m tired, but there’s this thing in my head about the forms or the schedule or someone not charting right, and then I’m up again. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s actually less sleep or just bad sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like really sharp. Because in that moment it feels like they’re stopping me from doing something important. It’s more like I’ll have these stretches where I feel like I’m finally awake and everything is obvious, like at work I can see what needs fixing and I’m the one who’s supposed to handle it.\n\nAnd then if my husband says I’m moving too fast, or one of the nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap. Like they’re getting in the way of something important. It’s embarrassing after, because I’m not usually like that… but in the moment it feels like they don’t understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… the racing part, definitely. It’s like my brain won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be thinking about a wound measurement, and then suddenly it’s patient initials, then dates on the forms, then what it all means, and then whether someone missed something—like ten tabs open at once and none of them will close.\n\nAnd I try to explain it, but then I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and it probably comes out all jumbled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these times when you're extremely awake and can see what's wrong are separate episodes, or is it more of a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: It feels more like separate times, I think. Like I can be mostly normal, doing my usual routine, and then some days it’s like a switch flips and I’m finally awake and I can see all the things that are wrong at work and I have to fix them.\n\nAnd then—this is where it gets messy—if my husband says I need to slow down, or one of the nurses tells me I’m doing too much, I just snap. I don’t mean to turn it into a whole thing, but in that moment it feels like I’m the only one seeing it clearly.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel extremely awake and can see what's wrong, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Sleep is… it’s hard to separate out, because some nights I’m just up thinking about work stuff or what I forgot to do, and then other nights I crash early because I’m exhausted from being on my feet all day.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I need less sleep exactly. My husband might have a better answer because he notices things I don’t, like if I’m pacing around or doing laundry at weird times—well, not laundry, I mean sometimes I’ll start sorting things and then remember there’s a uniform shirt missing… sorry. I guess I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep on the days when you feel extremely awake and like you can see what's wrong at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it less sleep. It’s more like… I don’t notice sleep the same way on those days, if that makes sense. I’ll be thinking about work and what needs to be corrected, and then I look at the clock and it’s later than I thought.\n\nBut then sometimes I still feel tired, or I’ll tell myself I’m tired, but there’s this thing in my head about the forms or the schedule or someone not charting right, and then I’m up again. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s actually less sleep or just bad sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy or elevated and had increased energy for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Happy, I don’t know… I wouldn’t say happy like going on vacation or something.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I function normally, exactly. I mean, at work I’ll be doing things and moving around, and sometimes I feel like I’m keeping up better than everyone else, but then “normal” is hard to say because there’s always something going wrong on the floor.

Like last week—well, not last week maybe, but one of those shifts—there were call lights and trays and someone had left supplies in the wrong place, and I was trying to straighten that out, and then I don’t even remember if I’d slept much the night before. I just kept going.

So… I don’t know if it’s less sleep and functioning normally, or if I’m just pushing through because I feel like I have to. My husband would probably say I’m not acting normal, but I don’t always see it that way at the time.
